Yeah, hi. So, um, I guess I'm here because things have just been really... well, just a lot lately. You see, my sister recently moved back into our house, and she brought her new baby with her. And, like, don't get me wrong, I love the kid, but ever since they got there, the whole house is just upside down. I’ve been way more on edge than usual, and honestly, just feeling kind of down. I find myself snapping at everyone over the stupidest little stuff at the house—like, just tiny things that never used to bother me. 

And because of all that, by the time I actually get to my college classes or show up at the mechanic shop... I'm training to be an industrial machinery mechanic, so it's a lot of heavy, precise work... I'm just completely drained before the day even starts. I mean, I'm still getting by, I'm not totally failing out or anything, but I have missed a couple of deadlines lately. I even had to back out of helping build the set for the theater department. I usually help them out with the rigging and the frames and stuff, but I just couldn't keep everything straight in my head anymore with all the chaos going on at home, so I just had to drop it. It's just been really overwhelming trying to balance the shop, the baby crying, the classes...

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, it's definitely not that. I still really, really like doing the theater sets. Like, honestly, that's usually the best part of my week. Last semester we built this massive, two-story rig for a play, and I was the one welding the steel frames and making sure the bracing was right so it wouldn't sway when people walked on it. I love that stuff, I really do. So the interest is 100 percent still there. 

It's just... man, it's just the bandwidth, you know? Like, with my sister's baby crying all night—I swear he's got colic or something because it's just constant, right through the drywall—I'm getting zero sleep. And when you're working with industrial machinery, like lathes and heavy presses in my main classes, you can't be zoning out. You make one mistake there and you lose a finger, or worse. So I have to use every single ounce of focus I have just to get through my required shop hours safely. 

By the time the afternoon rolls around and it's time to head over to the theater to do the fun stuff, my brain is just total mush. I actually really wanted to help them this month because they're setting up fly rigs, which is super cool and actually relates to the industrial rigging I'm learning, but I just had to tell the tech director I couldn't do it. I knew if I went in there, I'd end up messing up a measurement or forgetting to secure a bolt just because my head is so foggy from everything going on at the house. So, yeah, I haven't lost interest at all. I still want to do it. I just... physically and mentally don't have the gas in the tank right now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Flashbacks? No, no, nothing like that. I haven't been through anything traumatic like a car crash or a war zone or anything crazy, so there's really nothing for me to have a flashback about, you know? 

And as for nightmares, I mean, I don't really get those either. Well, I guess sometimes I'll have just weird, annoying dreams, like... you know when you're super stressed and you dream about being late for a final exam, or, like, for me, I'll dream about dropping a heavy wrench into a running gearbox or messing up a weld? But it's definitely not like I'm waking up in a cold sweat terrified or reliving some horrible event. It's just normal, stupid stress brain stuff. Honestly, I'd have to actually be asleep long enough to have a real nightmare anyway, which, like I said, isn't really happening with the baby crying all night.

As for feeling on high alert... I mean, I guess I am a little jumpy right now, but again, it's not because of some past trauma. It's literally just because I'm constantly waiting for the kid to start screaming again. Like, I'll be sitting on the couch downstairs trying to read my textbook on hydraulic systems, and I'll hear a floorboard creak upstairs, and my shoulders just immediately tense up right to my ears. Because I'm just sitting there thinking, "Oh great, here we go, he's awake and he's gonna start wailing for the next three hours, and I have to be at the shop at 7 AM." So I am on edge, for sure, but it's strictly just anticipating the noise and the chaos in the house. It's not like I'm reliving anything from the past. I'm just trying to survive the current mess in my living room, you know?

I actually really wanted to help them this month because they're setting up fly rigs, which is super cool and actually relates to the industrial rigging I'm learning, but I just had to tell the tech director I couldn't do it. I knew if I went in there, I'd end up messing up a measurement or forgetting to secure a bolt just because my head is so foggy from everything going on at the house. I haven't been through anything traumatic like a car crash or a war zone or anything crazy, so there's really nothing for me to have a flashback about, you know? \n\nAnd as for nightmares, I mean, I don't really get those either. Well, I guess sometimes I'll have just weird, annoying dreams, like... you know when you're super stressed and you dream about being late for a final exam, or, like, for me, I'll dream about dropping a heavy wrench into a running gearbox or messing up a weld? Because I'm just sitting there thinking, \"Oh great, here we go, he's awake and he's gonna start wailing for the next three hours, and I have to be at the shop at 7 AM.\" So I am on edge, for sure, but it's strictly just anticipating the noise and the chaos in the house. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, like I was kind of saying a minute ago, it's definitely not that I don't enjoy it anymore. The pleasure is totally still there. Like, if I actually had the energy to go in and work on those sets, I'd be having a blast. I still get a huge kick out of figuring out the mechanics of it all.

My brain still lights up for that stuff, you know? I still get that same rush when I figure out a structural problem or when a weld comes out perfectly clean. It's not like things have gone gray or boring for me. I'm not sitting around staring at the wall feeling empty or whatever. 

It's literally just the physical exhaustion. If my sister and her baby moved out tomorrow and I could finally get a full eight hours of sleep without waking up to screaming, I'd be right back in the theater department the very next afternoon, having the time of my life. So, yeah, the enjoyment hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... it's just buried under all this fatigue right now. I just physically don't have the stamina to go do the things I know I still love doing.

I usually help them out with the rigging and the frames and stuff, but I just couldn't keep everything straight in my head anymore with all the chaos going on at home, so I just had to drop it. It's just been really overwhelming trying to balance the shop, the baby crying, the classes...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like building sets for the theater department or other activities?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's definitely not that. I still really, really like doing the theater sets. Like, honestly, that's usually the best part of my week. Last semester we built this massive, two-story rig for a play, and I was the one welding the steel frames and making sure the bracing was right so it wouldn't sway when people walked on it. I love that stuff, I really do. So the interest is 100 percent still there. \n\nIt's just... man, it's just the bandwidth, you know? Like, with my sister's baby crying all night—I swear he's got colic or something because it's just constant, right through the drywall—I'm getting zero sleep. And when you're working with industrial machinery, like lathes and heavy presses in my main classes, you can't be zoning out. You make one mistake there and you lose a finger, or worse. So I have to use every single ounce of focus I have just to get through my required shop hours safely. \n\nBy the time the afternoon rolls around and it's time to head over to the theater to do the fun stuff, my brain is just total mush. I actually really wanted to help them this month because they're setting up fly rigs, which is super cool and actually relates to the industrial rigging I'm learning, but I just had to tell the tech director I couldn't do it. I knew if I went in there, I'd end up messing up a measurement or forgetting to secure a bolt just because my head is so foggy from everything going on at the house. So, yeah, I haven't lost interest at all. I still want to do it. I just... physically and mentally don't have the gas in the tank right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having flashbacks, nightmares, or feeling on high alert like you're reliving a stressful or traumatic event?\n\nPatient: Flashbacks? No, no, nothing like that. I haven't been through anything traumatic like a car crash or a war zone or anything crazy, so there's really nothing for me to have a flashback about, you know? \n\nAnd as for nightmares, I mean, I don't really get those either. Well, I guess sometimes I'll have just weird, annoying dreams, like... you know when you're super stressed and you dream about being late for a final exam, or, like, for me, I'll dream about dropping a heavy wrench into a running gearbox or messing up a weld? But it's definitely not like I'm waking up in a cold sweat terrified or reliving some horrible event. It's just normal, stupid stress brain stuff. Honestly, I'd have to actually be asleep long enough to have a real nightmare anyway, which, like I said, isn't really happening with the baby crying all night.\n\nAs for feeling on high alert... I mean, I guess I am a little jumpy right now, but again, it's not because of some past trauma. It's literally just because I'm constantly waiting for the kid to start screaming again. Like, I'll be sitting on the couch downstairs trying to read my textbook on hydraulic systems, and I'll hear a floorboard creak upstairs, and my shoulders just immediately tense up right to my ears. Because I'm just sitting there thinking, \"Oh great, here we go, he's awake and he's gonna start wailing for the next three hours, and I have to be at the shop at 7 AM.\" So I am on edge, for sure, but it's strictly just anticipating the noise and the chaos in the house. It's not like I'm reliving anything from the past. I'm just trying to survive the current mess in my living room, you know?\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you found that you no longer take pleasure in activities, even those you used to enjoy, like building theater sets?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was kind of saying a minute ago, it's definitely not that I don't enjoy it anymore. The pleasure is totally still there. Like, if I actually had the energy to go in and work on those sets, I'd be having a blast. I still get a huge kick out of figuring out the mechanics of it all. \n\nJust the other day, one of the tech guys texted me a picture of the new rigging they're trying to set up for the spring musical, and I immediately started thinking about the load limits and how they should brace the corner joints so it doesn't buckle. My brain still lights up for that stuff, you know? I still get that same rush when I figure out a structural problem or when a weld comes out perfectly clean. It's not like things have gone gray or boring for me. I'm not sitting around staring at the wall feeling empty or whatever. \n\nIt's literally just the physical exhaustion. If my sister and her baby moved out tomorrow and I could finally get a full eight hours of sleep without waking up to screaming, I'd be right back in the theater department the very next afternoon, having the time of my life. So, yeah, the enjoyment hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... it's just buried under all this fatigue right now. I just physically don't have the stamina to go do the things I know I still love doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I feel like maybe I'm just not explaining it right. It's not that the satisfaction is gone. Like, not at all. If I actually get my hands on a project, it feels exactly as good as it always did. 

Like, okay, for example, a couple weekends ago, right? Before things got totally out of hand with the baby's sleep schedule. I had a Saturday morning where, by some absolute miracle, the kid slept for like four hours straight the night before. I woke up feeling somewhat human, and I went into the shop to work on this independent project I've been messing around with—a custom gear assembly for this little motorized cart I'm trying to build. And I spent three hours just machining these tiny, intricate parts on the lathe. Making sure the tolerances were absolutely perfect, like down to the millimeter. And when I finally fit them together and they meshed perfectly without any grinding or catching... man, that feeling was incredible. It was just as satisfying, just as rewarding as it was six months ago before my sister moved in. I was riding that high all day. I even took a video of the gears turning and sent it to my buddy because I was so proud of it. 

So the reward is still there. The dopamine hit, or whatever you want to call it, is definitely still firing. The problem is just that those moments where I actually have the energy to get started are so incredibly rare right now. It's the getting started part that's the hurdle. It's like... imagine you have your favorite meal sitting right in front of you, like a massive, perfectly cooked steak, but you just ran a marathon and you're so exhausted you can't even lift the fork to your mouth. The steak still tastes amazing, you still want it, but the physical act of eating it is just too much effort. That's where I'm at. When I do manage to lift the fork—when I do manage to get into the shop or help out with a theater set—it's incredibly satisfying. I just don't have the strength to lift the fork very often these days. Does that make sense? Because I really want to make sure I'm being clear that the passion for the work hasn't faded one bit.

If my sister and her baby moved out tomorrow and I could finally get a full eight hours of sleep without waking up to screaming, I'd be right back in the theater department the very next afternoon, having the time of my life. So, yeah, the enjoyment hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... it's just buried under all this fatigue right now. I just physically don't have the stamina to go do the things I know I still love doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you do have the energy, the things you used to really enjoy—like building sets or working with machinery—just don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\nPatient: No, no, I feel like maybe I'm just not explaining it right. It's not that the satisfaction is gone. Like, not at all. If I actually get my hands on a project, it feels exactly as good as it always did. \n\nLike, okay, for example, a couple weekends ago, right? Before things got totally out of hand with the baby's sleep schedule. I had a Saturday morning where, by some absolute miracle, the kid slept for like four hours straight the night before. I woke up feeling somewhat human, and I went into the shop to work on this independent project I've been messing around with—a custom gear assembly for this little motorized cart I'm trying to build. And I spent three hours just machining these tiny, intricate parts on the lathe. Making sure the tolerances were absolutely perfect, like down to the millimeter. And when I finally fit them together and they meshed perfectly without any grinding or catching... man, that feeling was incredible. It was just as satisfying, just as rewarding as it was six months ago before my sister moved in. I was riding that high all day. I even took a video of the gears turning and sent it to my buddy because I was so proud of it. \n\nSo the reward is still there. The dopamine hit, or whatever you want to call it, is definitely still firing. The problem is just that those moments where I actually have the energy to get started are so incredibly rare right now. It's the getting started part that's the hurdle. It's like... imagine you have your favorite meal sitting right in front of you, like a massive, perfectly cooked steak, but you just ran a marathon and you're so exhausted you can't even lift the fork to your mouth. The steak still tastes amazing, you still want it, but the physical act of eating it is just too much effort. That's where I'm at. When I do manage to lift the fork—when I do manage to get into the shop or help out with a theater set—it's incredibly satisfying. I just don't have the strength to lift the fork very often these days. Does that make sense? Because I really want to make sure I'm being clear that the passion for the work hasn't faded one bit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, no, definitely not. If I actually get sleep, I'm totally fine. Like, I'm just a normal eighteen-year-old guy with normal energy. It's literally a one-to-one ratio—if I sleep, I have energy; if I don't, I'm a zombie. 

Take last Tuesday, for example. My sister actually took the baby over to our aunt's house overnight because she knew I had a huge midterm in my pneumatics class on Wednesday morning and I was kind of losing my mind about it. So the house was completely, beautifully, dead quiet for the first time in what feels like a century. I went to bed at like 10:30, out like a light, and I didn't wake up until my alarm went off at 6 AM. 

And man, let me tell you, when I woke up, I felt like a completely different person. I didn't even hit snooze. I just popped right out of bed, made my usual cup of coffee—just one regular cup, not the entire pot like I've been having to drink lately just to keep my eyes open—and I felt great. I went to my midterm, crushed it, and then I went straight to the shop and worked a full shift doing maintenance on this massive industrial conveyor belt system we've been overhauling. And I wasn't dragging at all. I was crawling under the rollers, torqueing bolts, hauling heavy gear around, and I felt completely normal. Honestly, I felt like Superman just because I hadn't felt that rested in weeks. 

So, yeah, it's definitely not like I have some mysterious fatigue or something wrong with my body where I'm tired for no reason. If the kid screams and I sleep for two hours, I can barely lift a wrench. If the kid is quiet and I get my seven or eight hours, I'm at a hundred percent, ready to go. There's no lingering tiredness if I actually get the rest. It's just that getting the rest is basically a lottery right now, you know? But the energy is totally there when the sleep is there.

I went to bed at like 10:30, out like a light, and I didn't wake up until my alarm went off at 6 AM. \n\nAnd man, let me tell you, when I woke up, I felt like a completely different person. I didn't even hit snooze. I just popped right out of bed, made my usual cup of coffee—just one regular cup, not the entire pot like I've been having to drink lately just to keep my eyes open—and I felt great. I went to my midterm, crushed it, and then I went straight to the shop and worked a full shift doing maintenance on this massive industrial conveyor belt system we've been overhauling. And I wasn't dragging at all. I was crawling under the rollers, torqueing bolts, hauling heavy gear around, and I felt completely normal. Honestly, I felt like Superman just because I hadn't felt that rested in weeks. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely not like I have some mysterious fatigue or something wrong with my body where I'm tired for no reason. If the kid screams and I sleep for two hours, I can barely lift a wrench. If the kid is quiet and I get my seven or eight hours, I'm at a hundred percent, ready to go. There's no lingering tiredness if I actually get the rest. It's just that getting the rest is basically a lottery right now, you know? But the energy is totally there when the sleep is there.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any days—whether recently or in the past few weeks—where even when you had the chance to do something you enjoy, like working on machinery or building sets, you didn’t feel like doing it at all?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, honestly, no. I mean... I feel like we're kind of going in circles here, but no, that just doesn't happen to me. The drive to do it is always there. Always. I never just sit there with a free afternoon and a chance to work on something and think, 'Nah, I don't really feel like it today.' That just isn't how I'm wired. 

Like, just this past weekend, my buddy called me up because his '98 Civic was having this weird alternator issue, right? And normally, you know, I'm more of an industrial machinery guy—conveyors, pneumatics, heavy-duty refractory stuff—but an engine is an engine, and troubleshooting is troubleshooting. And even though I was absolutely dead on my feet from my niece crying literally all night on Friday, the second he asked if I wanted to come over and look at it, my brain was like, 'Yes, absolutely.' I *wanted* to go. I didn't have that feeling of, like, apathy or whatever. I cared a lot! I was already running through the diagnostic steps in my head while I was on the phone with him, thinking about checking the belt tension, testing the voltage drop across the battery terminals, visualizing the whole process. 

I actually ended up having to tell him no, which sucked, but only because I physically couldn't keep my eyes open to drive over to his place safely. But the *desire* was a hundred percent there. It honestly really bummed me out to have to pass on it. I never, ever lose that feeling of wanting to get my hands dirty and fix things. If the opportunity is there, my brain wants to jump on it. It's just my body giving out, not my interest. I don't know if I'm just repeating myself at this point, but it's like... my mind is always, always hungry for the work, even if my body is running on empty. So, no, I never have days where I just don't feel like doing it.

So I have to use every single ounce of focus I have just to get through my required shop hours safely. \n\nBy the time the afternoon rolls around and it's time to head over to the theater to do the fun stuff, my brain is just total mush. I actually really wanted to help them this month because they're setting up fly rigs, which is super cool and actually relates to the industrial rigging I'm learning, but I just had to tell the tech director I couldn't do it. I knew if I went in there, I'd end up messing up a measurement or forgetting to secure a bolt just because my head is so foggy from everything going on at the house. So, yeah, I haven't lost interest at all. I still want to do it. I just... physically and mentally don't have the gas in the tank right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having flashbacks, nightmares, or feeling on high alert like you're reliving a stressful or traumatic event?\n\nPatient: Flashbacks? No, no, nothing like that. I haven't been through anything traumatic like a car crash or a war zone or anything crazy, so there's really nothing for me to have a flashback about, you know? \n\nAnd as for nightmares, I mean, I don't really get those either. Well, I guess sometimes I'll have just weird, annoying dreams, like... you know when you're super stressed and you dream about being late for a final exam, or, like, for me, I'll dream about dropping a heavy wrench into a running gearbox or messing up a weld? But it's definitely not like I'm waking up in a cold sweat terrified or reliving some horrible event. It's just normal, stupid stress brain stuff. Honestly, I'd have to actually be asleep long enough to have a real nightmare anyway, which, like I said, isn't really happening with the baby crying all night.\n\nAs for feeling on high alert... I mean, I guess I am a little jumpy right now, but again, it's not because of some past trauma. It's literally just because I'm constantly waiting for the kid to start screaming again. Like, I'll be sitting on the couch downstairs trying to read my textbook on hydraulic systems, and I'll hear a floorboard creak upstairs, and my shoulders just immediately tense up right to my ears. Because I'm just sitting there thinking, \"Oh great, here we go, he's awake and he's gonna start wailing for the next three hours, and I have to be at the shop at 7 AM.\" So I am on edge, for sure, but it's strictly just anticipating the noise and the chaos in the house. It's not like I'm reliving anything from the past. I'm just trying to survive the current mess in my living room, you know?\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you found that you no longer take pleasure in activities, even those you used to enjoy, like building theater sets?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was kind of saying a minute ago, it's definitely not that I don't enjoy it anymore. The pleasure is totally still there. Like, if I actually had the energy to go in and work on those sets, I'd be having a blast. I still get a huge kick out of figuring out the mechanics of it all. \n\nJust the other day, one of the tech guys texted me a picture of the new rigging they're trying to set up for the spring musical, and I immediately started thinking about the load limits and how they should brace the corner joints so it doesn't buckle. My brain still lights up for that stuff, you know? I still get that same rush when I figure out a structural problem or when a weld comes out perfectly clean. It's not like things have gone gray or boring for me. I'm not sitting around staring at the wall feeling empty or whatever. \n\nIt's literally just the physical exhaustion. If my sister and her baby moved out tomorrow and I could finally get a full eight hours of sleep without waking up to screaming, I'd be right back in the theater department the very next afternoon, having the time of my life. So, yeah, the enjoyment hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... it's just buried under all this fatigue right now. I just physically don't have the stamina to go do the things I know I still love doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you do have the energy, the things you used to really enjoy—like building sets or working with machinery—just don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\nPatient: No, no, I feel like maybe I'm just not explaining it right. It's not that the satisfaction is gone. Like, not at all. If I actually get my hands on a project, it feels exactly as good as it always did. \n\nLike, okay, for example, a couple weekends ago, right? Before things got totally out of hand with the baby's sleep schedule. I had a Saturday morning where, by some absolute miracle, the kid slept for like four hours straight the night before. I woke up feeling somewhat human, and I went into the shop to work on this independent project I've been messing around with—a custom gear assembly for this little motorized cart I'm trying to build. And I spent three hours just machining these tiny, intricate parts on the lathe. Making sure the tolerances were absolutely perfect, like down to the millimeter. And when I finally fit them together and they meshed perfectly without any grinding or catching... man, that feeling was incredible. It was just as satisfying, just as rewarding as it was six months ago before my sister moved in. I was riding that high all day. I even took a video of the gears turning and sent it to my buddy because I was so proud of it. \n\nSo the reward is still there. The dopamine hit, or whatever you want to call it, is definitely still firing. The problem is just that those moments where I actually have the energy to get started are so incredibly rare right now. It's the getting started part that's the hurdle. It's like... imagine you have your favorite meal sitting right in front of you, like a massive, perfectly cooked steak, but you just ran a marathon and you're so exhausted you can't even lift the fork to your mouth. The steak still tastes amazing, you still want it, but the physical act of eating it is just too much effort. That's where I'm at. When I do manage to lift the fork—when I do manage to get into the shop or help out with a theater set—it's incredibly satisfying. I just don't have the strength to lift the fork very often these days. Does that make sense? Because I really want to make sure I'm being clear that the passion for the work hasn't faded one bit.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when the baby didn’t keep you up much and you got a decent amount of sleep, have you still felt unusually tired or like you don’t have much energy?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not. If I actually get sleep, I'm totally fine. Like, I'm just a normal eighteen-year-old guy with normal energy. It's literally a one-to-one ratio—if I sleep, I have energy; if I don't, I'm a zombie. \n\nTake last Tuesday, for example. My sister actually took the baby over to our aunt's house overnight because she knew I had a huge midterm in my pneumatics class on Wednesday morning and I was kind of losing my mind about it. So the house was completely, beautifully, dead quiet for the first time in what feels like a century. I went to bed at like 10:30, out like a light, and I didn't wake up until my alarm went off at 6 AM. \n\nAnd man, let me tell you, when I woke up, I felt like a completely different person. I didn't even hit snooze. I just popped right out of bed, made my usual cup of coffee—just one regular cup, not the entire pot like I've been having to drink lately just to keep my eyes open—and I felt great. I went to my midterm, crushed it, and then I went straight to the shop and worked a full shift doing maintenance on this massive industrial conveyor belt system we've been overhauling. And I wasn't dragging at all. I was crawling under the rollers, torqueing bolts, hauling heavy gear around, and I felt completely normal. Honestly, I felt like Superman just because I hadn't felt that rested in weeks. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely not like I have some mysterious fatigue or something wrong with my body where I'm tired for no reason. If the kid screams and I sleep for two hours, I can barely lift a wrench. If the kid is quiet and I get my seven or eight hours, I'm at a hundred percent, ready to go. There's no lingering tiredness if I actually get the rest. It's just that getting the rest is basically a lottery right now, you know? But the energy is totally there when the sleep is there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any days—whether recently or in the past few weeks—where even when you had the chance to do something you enjoy, like working on machinery or building sets, you didn’t feel like doing it at all?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. I mean... I feel like we're kind of going in circles here, but no, that just doesn't happen to me. The drive to do it is always there. Always. I never just sit there with a free afternoon and a chance to work on something and think, 'Nah, I don't really feel like it today.' That just isn't how I'm wired. \n\nLike, just this past weekend, my buddy called me up because his '98 Civic was having this weird alternator issue, right? And normally, you know, I'm more of an industrial machinery guy—conveyors, pneumatics, heavy-duty refractory stuff—but an engine is an engine, and troubleshooting is troubleshooting. And even though I was absolutely dead on my feet from my niece crying literally all night on Friday, the second he asked if I wanted to come over and look at it, my brain was like, 'Yes, absolutely.' I *wanted* to go. I didn't have that feeling of, like, apathy or whatever. I cared a lot! I was already running through the diagnostic steps in my head while I was on the phone with him, thinking about checking the belt tension, testing the voltage drop across the battery terminals, visualizing the whole process. \n\nI actually ended up having to tell him no, which sucked, but only because I physically couldn't keep my eyes open to drive over to his place safely. But the *desire* was a hundred percent there. It honestly really bummed me out to have to pass on it. I never, ever lose that feeling of wanting to get my hands dirty and fix things. If the opportunity is there, my brain wants to jump on it. It's just my body giving out, not my interest. I don't know if I'm just repeating myself at this point, but it's like... my mind is always, always hungry for the work, even if my body is running on empty. So, no, I never have days where I just don't feel like doing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... doc, I feel like I’m kind of a broken record here. I know you have to ask all the variations of this, but no. Never. If I am not physically tired, I care just as much as I always have. There is never a time where I have the energy but just lack the... the passion, I guess you'd call it. 

Like, if I get a full night's sleep, I am literally the first guy in the shop. I'll be in there before my shift even officially starts, just organizing my tool chest, checking the calibration on my micrometers, looking for something to turn a wrench on. I work with heavy-duty stuff—refractory machinery, kilns, massive industrial setups—and you really have to be dialed in. And I *love* being dialed in. 

Just Thursday, I had a gap between my classes, and because my aunt had taken the baby again Wednesday night, I was actually feeling rested. I didn't just sit around on my phone in the cafeteria feeling like I didn't care. I practically sprinted to the lab to work on this pneumatic sequencing circuit we’re building. I was so excited to figure out this issue we were having with the directional control valves. I spent two straight hours just mapping out the airflow, testing the actuators, getting grease all over my hands, and I was loving every single second of it. I care *so* much about this stuff. It's literally what I want to do with the rest of my life. 

So, no, if the physical exhaustion isn't weighing me down, the interest is at a hundred percent. I don't ever just sit around feeling empty or apathetic about my work. I care about it more than anything. It's literally just the fact that my sister's baby is keeping me awake that's the problem, not my brain or my attitude. If I have the energy, I'm all in. Every time.

But it's definitely not like I'm waking up in a cold sweat terrified or reliving some horrible event. It's just normal, stupid stress brain stuff. Like, I'll be sitting on the couch downstairs trying to read my textbook on hydraulic systems, and I'll hear a floorboard creak upstairs, and my shoulders just immediately tense up right to my ears. Because I'm just sitting there thinking, \"Oh great, here we go, he's awake and he's gonna start wailing for the next three hours, and I have to be at the shop at 7 AM.\" So I am on edge, for sure, but it's strictly just anticipating the noise and the chaos in the house. It's not like I'm reliving anything from the past. I'm just trying to survive the current mess in my living room, you know?\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you found that you no longer take pleasure in activities, even those you used to enjoy, like building theater sets?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was kind of saying a minute ago, it's definitely not that I don't enjoy it anymore. The pleasure is totally still there. Like, if I actually had the energy to go in and work on those sets, I'd be having a blast. I still get a huge kick out of figuring out the mechanics of it all. \n\nJust the other day, one of the tech guys texted me a picture of the new rigging they're trying to set up for the spring musical, and I immediately started thinking about the load limits and how they should brace the corner joints so it doesn't buckle. My brain still lights up for that stuff, you know? I still get that same rush when I figure out a structural problem or when a weld comes out perfectly clean. It's not like things have gone gray or boring for me. I'm not sitting around staring at the wall feeling empty or whatever. \n\nIt's literally just the physical exhaustion. If my sister and her baby moved out tomorrow and I could finally get a full eight hours of sleep without waking up to screaming, I'd be right back in the theater department the very next afternoon, having the time of my life. So, yeah, the enjoyment hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... it's just buried under all this fatigue right now. I just physically don't have the stamina to go do the things I know I still love doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you do have the energy, the things you used to really enjoy—like building sets or working with machinery—just don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\nPatient: No, no, I feel like maybe I'm just not explaining it right. It's not that the satisfaction is gone. Like, not at all. If I actually get my hands on a project, it feels exactly as good as it always did. \n\nLike, okay, for example, a couple weekends ago, right? Before things got totally out of hand with the baby's sleep schedule. I had a Saturday morning where, by some absolute miracle, the kid slept for like four hours straight the night before. I woke up feeling somewhat human, and I went into the shop to work on this independent project I've been messing around with—a custom gear assembly for this little motorized cart I'm trying to build. And I spent three hours just machining these tiny, intricate parts on the lathe. Making sure the tolerances were absolutely perfect, like down to the millimeter. And when I finally fit them together and they meshed perfectly without any grinding or catching... man, that feeling was incredible. It was just as satisfying, just as rewarding as it was six months ago before my sister moved in. I was riding that high all day. I even took a video of the gears turning and sent it to my buddy because I was so proud of it. \n\nSo the reward is still there. The dopamine hit, or whatever you want to call it, is definitely still firing. The problem is just that those moments where I actually have the energy to get started are so incredibly rare right now. It's the getting started part that's the hurdle. It's like... imagine you have your favorite meal sitting right in front of you, like a massive, perfectly cooked steak, but you just ran a marathon and you're so exhausted you can't even lift the fork to your mouth. The steak still tastes amazing, you still want it, but the physical act of eating it is just too much effort. That's where I'm at. When I do manage to lift the fork—when I do manage to get into the shop or help out with a theater set—it's incredibly satisfying. I just don't have the strength to lift the fork very often these days. Does that make sense? Because I really want to make sure I'm being clear that the passion for the work hasn't faded one bit.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when the baby didn’t keep you up much and you got a decent amount of sleep, have you still felt unusually tired or like you don’t have much energy?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not. If I actually get sleep, I'm totally fine. Like, I'm just a normal eighteen-year-old guy with normal energy. It's literally a one-to-one ratio—if I sleep, I have energy; if I don't, I'm a zombie. \n\nTake last Tuesday, for example. My sister actually took the baby over to our aunt's house overnight because she knew I had a huge midterm in my pneumatics class on Wednesday morning and I was kind of losing my mind about it. So the house was completely, beautifully, dead quiet for the first time in what feels like a century. I went to bed at like 10:30, out like a light, and I didn't wake up until my alarm went off at 6 AM. \n\nAnd man, let me tell you, when I woke up, I felt like a completely different person. I didn't even hit snooze. I just popped right out of bed, made my usual cup of coffee—just one regular cup, not the entire pot like I've been having to drink lately just to keep my eyes open—and I felt great. I went to my midterm, crushed it, and then I went straight to the shop and worked a full shift doing maintenance on this massive industrial conveyor belt system we've been overhauling. And I wasn't dragging at all. I was crawling under the rollers, torqueing bolts, hauling heavy gear around, and I felt completely normal. Honestly, I felt like Superman just because I hadn't felt that rested in weeks. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely not like I have some mysterious fatigue or something wrong with my body where I'm tired for no reason. If the kid screams and I sleep for two hours, I can barely lift a wrench. If the kid is quiet and I get my seven or eight hours, I'm at a hundred percent, ready to go. There's no lingering tiredness if I actually get the rest. It's just that getting the rest is basically a lottery right now, you know? But the energy is totally there when the sleep is there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any days—whether recently or in the past few weeks—where even when you had the chance to do something you enjoy, like working on machinery or building sets, you didn’t feel like doing it at all?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. I mean... I feel like we're kind of going in circles here, but no, that just doesn't happen to me. The drive to do it is always there. Always. I never just sit there with a free afternoon and a chance to work on something and think, 'Nah, I don't really feel like it today.' That just isn't how I'm wired. \n\nLike, just this past weekend, my buddy called me up because his '98 Civic was having this weird alternator issue, right? But the *desire* was a hundred percent there. It honestly really bummed me out to have to pass on it. I never, ever lose that feeling of wanting to get my hands dirty and fix things. If the opportunity is there, my brain wants to jump on it. It's just my body giving out, not my interest. I don't know if I'm just repeating myself at this point, but it's like... my mind is always, always hungry for the work, even if my body is running on empty. So, no, I never have days where I just don't feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don’t care about doing the things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not physically tired?\n\nPatient: I mean... doc, I feel like I’m kind of a broken record here. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Okay, Doc, I really don't mean to sound disrespectful, but I feel like we are kind of beating a dead horse here. I know you've got a checklist or whatever, but the answer is still no. Never. Not even for a second. If I'm awake and rested, starting a project is literally the easiest thing in the world for me. I never have that thing where you stare at a job and think, 'Ugh, I just can't get into it,' or 'That's way too much effort.' 

Let me give you a perfect example. A couple of weeks ago, right before my niece's sleep schedule went completely radioactive and I actually had a decent night's sleep, I had a Saturday off. I wasn't tired at all. I had this old two-stage air compressor sitting in my garage that I'd bought off Craigslist for like fifty bucks. The guy who sold it to me said the motor was shot and the tank was probably compromised. Now, somebody who lacks excitement or thinks things are too much effort would just leave a heavy piece of junk like that sitting in the corner under a tarp for months, right? You'd look at it and go, 'Yeah, I'll get to it eventually.' 

But me? The second I woke up that morning—boom. I didn't even eat breakfast. I didn't sit around watching TV or trying to hype myself up to go work on it. I just threw on my coveralls, grabbed my socket set, and went straight out to the garage. I didn't drag my feet at all. I immediately started tearing down the pump assembly, inspecting the reed valves, checking the piston rings to see if they were totally fried. I was so amped to figure out exactly why it wasn't building pressure. I was in the zone, completely fired up about it. I spent six straight hours pulling that thing apart, cleaning out the gunk, mapping out what replacement parts I needed to machine, and I didn't even realize I was starving until my sister came out to ask if I was ever going to come inside and eat something. 

So, I really don't know how else to explain it, man. The excitement to start is always, always there. I don't need a warm-up period. I don't have to force myself to care. The only thing—literally the only thing—that ever stops me from diving headfirst into a project is if my eyelids are physically closing against my will because that baby kept me up until 4 AM. That's it. My brain never, ever says 'this is too much effort' when it comes to machines. It just doesn't happen.

I just physically don't have the stamina to go do the things I know I still love doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you do have the energy, the things you used to really enjoy—like building sets or working with machinery—just don’t feel as satisfying or rewarding as they used to?\n\nPatient: No, no, I feel like maybe I'm just not explaining it right. Before things got totally out of hand with the baby's sleep schedule. I had a Saturday morning where, by some absolute miracle, the kid slept for like four hours straight the night before. And I spent three hours just machining these tiny, intricate parts on the lathe. Making sure the tolerances were absolutely perfect, like down to the millimeter. And when I finally fit them together and they meshed perfectly without any grinding or catching... man, that feeling was incredible. It was just as satisfying, just as rewarding as it was six months ago before my sister moved in. I was riding that high all day. I even took a video of the gears turning and sent it to my buddy because I was so proud of it. \n\nSo the reward is still there. The dopamine hit, or whatever you want to call it, is definitely still firing. The problem is just that those moments where I actually have the energy to get started are so incredibly rare right now. It's the getting started part that's the hurdle. It's like... imagine you have your favorite meal sitting right in front of you, like a massive, perfectly cooked steak, but you just ran a marathon and you're so exhausted you can't even lift the fork to your mouth. The steak still tastes amazing, you still want it, but the physical act of eating it is just too much effort. That's where I'm at. When I do manage to lift the fork—when I do manage to get into the shop or help out with a theater set—it's incredibly satisfying. I just don't have the strength to lift the fork very often these days. Does that make sense? Because I really want to make sure I'm being clear that the passion for the work hasn't faded one bit.\n\nDoctor: Even on days when the baby didn’t keep you up much and you got a decent amount of sleep, have you still felt unusually tired or like you don’t have much energy?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not. If I actually get sleep, I'm totally fine. Like, I'm just a normal eighteen-year-old guy with normal energy. It's literally a one-to-one ratio—if I sleep, I have energy; if I don't, I'm a zombie. \n\nTake last Tuesday, for example. My sister actually took the baby over to our aunt's house overnight because she knew I had a huge midterm in my pneumatics class on Wednesday morning and I was kind of losing my mind about it. So the house was completely, beautifully, dead quiet for the first time in what feels like a century. I went to bed at like 10:30, out like a light, and I didn't wake up until my alarm went off at 6 AM. \n\nAnd man, let me tell you, when I woke up, I felt like a completely different person. I didn't even hit snooze. I just popped right out of bed, made my usual cup of coffee—just one regular cup, not the entire pot like I've been having to drink lately just to keep my eyes open—and I felt great. I went to my midterm, crushed it, and then I went straight to the shop and worked a full shift doing maintenance on this massive industrial conveyor belt system we've been overhauling. And I wasn't dragging at all. I was crawling under the rollers, torqueing bolts, hauling heavy gear around, and I felt completely normal. Honestly, I felt like Superman just because I hadn't felt that rested in weeks. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely not like I have some mysterious fatigue or something wrong with my body where I'm tired for no reason. If the kid screams and I sleep for two hours, I can barely lift a wrench. If the kid is quiet and I get my seven or eight hours, I'm at a hundred percent, ready to go. There's no lingering tiredness if I actually get the rest. It's just that getting the rest is basically a lottery right now, you know? But the energy is totally there when the sleep is there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any days—whether recently or in the past few weeks—where even when you had the chance to do something you enjoy, like working on machinery or building sets, you didn’t feel like doing it at all?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. I mean... I feel like we're kind of going in circles here, but no, that just doesn't happen to me. The drive to do it is always there. Always. I never just sit there with a free afternoon and a chance to work on something and think, 'Nah, I don't really feel like it today.' That just isn't how I'm wired. \n\nLike, just this past weekend, my buddy called me up because his '98 Civic was having this weird alternator issue, right? And normally, you know, I'm more of an industrial machinery guy—conveyors, pneumatics, heavy-duty refractory stuff—but an engine is an engine, and troubleshooting is troubleshooting. And even though I was absolutely dead on my feet from my niece crying literally all night on Friday, the second he asked if I wanted to come over and look at it, my brain was like, 'Yes, absolutely.' I *wanted* to go. I didn't have that feeling of, like, apathy or whatever. I cared a lot! I was already running through the diagnostic steps in my head while I was on the phone with him, thinking about checking the belt tension, testing the voltage drop across the battery terminals, visualizing the whole process. \n\nI actually ended up having to tell him no, which sucked, but only because I physically couldn't keep my eyes open to drive over to his place safely. But the *desire* was a hundred percent there. It honestly really bummed me out to have to pass on it. I never, ever lose that feeling of wanting to get my hands dirty and fix things. If the opportunity is there, my brain wants to jump on it. It's just my body giving out, not my interest. I don't know if I'm just repeating myself at this point, but it's like... my mind is always, always hungry for the work, even if my body is running on empty. So, no, I never have days where I just don't feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don’t care about doing the things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not physically tired?\n\nPatient: I mean... doc, I feel like I’m kind of a broken record here. There is never a time where I have the energy but just lack the... the passion, I guess you'd call it. \n\nLike, if I get a full night's sleep, I am literally the first guy in the shop. I'll be in there before my shift even officially starts, just organizing my tool chest, checking the calibration on my micrometers, looking for something to turn a wrench on. I work with heavy-duty stuff—refractory machinery, kilns, massive industrial setups—and you really have to be dialed in. And I *love* being dialed in. \n\nJust Thursday, I had a gap between my classes, and because my aunt had taken the baby again Wednesday night, I was actually feeling rested. I didn't just sit around on my phone in the cafeteria feeling like I didn't care. I practically sprinted to the lab to work on this pneumatic sequencing circuit we’re building. I was so excited to figure out this issue we were having with the directional control valves. I spent two straight hours just mapping out the airflow, testing the actuators, getting grease all over my hands, and I was loving every single second of it. I care *so* much about this stuff. I had this old two-stage air compressor sitting in my garage that I'd bought off Craigslist for like fifty bucks. The guy who sold it to me said the motor was shot and the tank was probably compromised. Now, somebody who lacks excitement or thinks things are too much effort would just leave a heavy piece of junk like that sitting in the corner under a tarp for months, right? I was so amped to figure out exactly why it wasn't building pressure. I was in the zone, completely fired up about it. I spent six straight hours pulling that thing apart, cleaning out the gunk, mapping out what replacement parts I needed to machine, and I didn't even realize I was starving until my sister came out to ask if I was ever going to come inside and eat something. \n\nSo, I really don't know how else to explain it, man. That just isn't how I'm wired. \n\nLike, just this past weekend, my buddy called me up because his '98 Civic was having this weird alternator issue, right? And normally, you know, I'm more of an industrial machinery guy—conveyors, pneumatics, heavy-duty refractory stuff—but an engine is an engine, and troubleshooting is troubleshooting. And even though I was absolutely dead on my feet from my niece crying literally all night on Friday, the second he asked if I wanted to come over and look at it, my brain was like, 'Yes, absolutely.' I *wanted* to go. I didn't have that feeling of, like, apathy or whatever. I cared a lot! I was already running through the diagnostic steps in my head while I was on the phone with him, thinking about checking the belt tension, testing the voltage drop across the battery terminals, visualizing the whole process. \n\nI actually ended up having to tell him no, which sucked, but only because I physically couldn't keep my eyes open to drive over to his place safely. But the *desire* was a hundred percent there. It honestly really bummed me out to have to pass on it. I never, ever lose that feeling of wanting to get my hands dirty and fix things. If the opportunity is there, my brain wants to jump on it. It's just my body giving out, not my interest. I don't know if I'm just repeating myself at this point, but it's like... my mind is always, always hungry for the work, even if my body is running on empty. So, no, I never have days where I just don't feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don’t care about doing the things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not physically tired?\n\nPatient: I mean... doc, I feel like I’m kind of a broken record here. I know you have to ask all the variations of this, but no. Never. If I am not physically tired, I care just as much as I always have. There is never a time where I have the energy but just lack the... the passion, I guess you'd call it. \n\nLike, if I get a full night's sleep, I am literally the first guy in the shop. I'll be in there before my shift even officially starts, just organizing my tool chest, checking the calibration on my micrometers, looking for something to turn a wrench on. I work with heavy-duty stuff—refractory machinery, kilns, massive industrial setups—and you really have to be dialed in. And I *love* being dialed in. \n\nJust Thursday, I had a gap between my classes, and because my aunt had taken the baby again Wednesday night, I was actually feeling rested. I didn't just sit around on my phone in the cafeteria feeling like I didn't care. I practically sprinted to the lab to work on this pneumatic sequencing circuit we’re building. I was so excited to figure out this issue we were having with the directional control valves. I spent two straight hours just mapping out the airflow, testing the actuators, getting grease all over my hands, and I was loving every single second of it. I care *so* much about this stuff. It's literally what I want to do with the rest of my life. \n\nSo, no, if the physical exhaustion isn't weighing me down, the interest is at a hundred percent. I don't ever just sit around feeling empty or apathetic about my work. I care about it more than anything. It's literally just the fact that my sister's baby is keeping me awake that's the problem, not my brain or my attitude. If I have the energy, I'm all in. Every time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like, even when you weren't physically tired, you just couldn't get yourself to start working on something you usually enjoy, like building sets or fixing machines, because it felt like too much effort or you couldn't get excited about it?\n\nPatient: Okay, Doc, I really don't mean to sound disrespectful, but I feel like we are kind of beating a dead horse here. I know you've got a checklist or whatever, but the answer is still no. Never. Not even for a second. If I'm awake and rested, starting a project is literally the easiest thing in the world for me. I never have that thing where you stare at a job and think, 'Ugh, I just can't get into it,' or 'That's way too much effort.' \n\nLet me give you a perfect example. A couple of weeks ago, right before my niece's sleep schedule went completely radioactive and I actually had a decent night's sleep, I had a Saturday off. I wasn't tired at all. I had this old two-stage air compressor sitting in my garage that I'd bought off Craigslist for like fifty bucks. The guy who sold it to me said the motor was shot and the tank was probably compromised. Now, somebody who lacks excitement or thinks things are too much effort would just leave a heavy piece of junk like that sitting in the corner under a tarp for months, right? You'd look at it and go, 'Yeah, I'll get to it eventually.' \n\nBut me? The second I woke up that morning—boom. I didn't even eat breakfast. I didn't sit around watching TV or trying to hype myself up to go work on it. I just threw on my coveralls, grabbed my socket set, and went straight out to the garage. I didn't drag my feet at all. I immediately started tearing down the pump assembly, inspecting the reed valves, checking the piston rings to see if they were totally fried. I was so amped to figure out exactly why it wasn't building pressure. I was in the zone, completely fired up about it. I spent six straight hours pulling that thing apart, cleaning out the gunk, mapping out what replacement parts I needed to machine, and I didn't even realize I was starving until my sister came out to ask if I was ever going to come inside and eat something. \n\nSo, I really don't know how else to explain it, man. The excitement to start is always, always there. I don't need a warm-up period. I don't have to force myself to care. The only thing—literally the only thing—that ever stops me from diving headfirst into a project is if my eyelids are physically closing against my will because that baby kept me up until 4 AM. That's it. My brain never, ever says 'this is too much effort' when it comes to machines.
